```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}
5	The foundations are established in \citet{carroll2006}, \citet{gustafson2004}, \citet{buonaccorsi2010}, and \citet{bound2001measurement}, with starred-node DAGs formalized by \citet{hernan_cole2009}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases toward the null~\cite{brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}, but this fails for polytomous exposures~\cite{dosemeci1990, jurek2005, veirod_laake2001}. \citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} showed dependent misclassification can bias in either direction, a concern that also drives mediation analyses with mismeasured mediators~\cite{valeri_vanderweele2013}. All analyze a single DAG topology; ours is the first to characterize how the \emph{same} confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven topologies.
6	
7	\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation.}
8	\citet{grimmer2022} and \citet{feder_etal2022} provide foundations for text-as-data and causal inference in NLP. \citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to classifier error; \citet{teblunthuis2024} gave the most complete binary treatment ($K{=}2$, four DAGs); \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only confounding. On annotation quality, \citet{gilardi2023chatgpt} and \citet{ziems2024can} document the rapid adoption of LLM annotators, \citet{baumann2025} found 31\% incorrect conclusions, \citet{pangakis_wolken_fasching2023} demonstrated that automated annotations require task-specific validation, \citet{cheng_mayya_sedoc2024} propose structured approaches to reduce LLM measurement error, and \citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\% error correlation across models. \citet{audinet2025} benchmark debiasing; \citet{keith_jensen_oconnor2020} survey the broader landscape. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose quality without connecting it to causal validity.
9	
10	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and correction methods.}
11	Our ASV framework draws on E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017}, the robustness value of \citet{cinelli2020making}, and the mediation sensitivity analysis of \citet{imai_keele_yamamoto2010}, but parametrizes annotation error severity rather than omitted variable strength or sequential ignorability violations. The unified measurement-error and missing-data framework of \citet{blackwell_honaker_king2017} similarly motivates treating annotation noise as a first-class object of inference. For correction, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic; \citet{zrnic_candes2024} extend PPI to gold-free settings, and \citet{gligoric2025} leverage LLM confidence scores. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four topologies. Bayesian measurement error models~\cite{richardson1993bayesian, gustafson2004} and EM imputation offer alternatives that bypass FWL invariance.
12	
```

</details>

The ratio isn't constant—it actually decreases as τ increases, suggesting ε scales inversely with τ rather than proportionally. But this is puzzling because the bias B(C) for confounding is defined as R(C)/D(C), where neither the numerator nor denominator should directly depend on τ, so I need to reconsider how τ affects the bias calculation in the confounding DGP. τ̂ depends on α and β through the FWL residual, not directly on τ.

```text
So the Conjecture's bound of ε < 0.06 (meaning less than 6% of τ) is violated at small τ values for confounding, mediation, and exposure—the relative error exceeds 6% when τ is around 0.1, even though it stays within bounds at larger τ like 0.5 or 1.0. Now I'm wondering whether the bias B actually changes when τ varies—if I keep the confounding structure (the effects of A on treatment and outcome, and the A distribution) constant, then B shouldn't depend on τ itself.

So the violations at τ = 5.0 are actually being normalized by τ_true = 0.5, which means the absolute error is closer to 0.07 rather than 0.139.

The key takeaway is that the ε < 0.06 bound isn't stable across all DGP parameters—confounding and mediation exceed it at small effect sizes (τ = 0.1 with max_ε up to 0.13), and front-door exceeds it at large β scaling (τ = 5.0 with max_ε around 0.139). ```text
```
```text
+
+\emph{Step 1: Decompose $T$ via the mediator.}
+Define $g(A)=\mathbb{E}[T\mid A]$ and $\varepsilon_T = T - g(A)$.
+By construction, $\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_T\mid A]=0$.
+By non-differential misclassification ($T\perp A^*\mid A$):
+$\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_T\mid A{=}k, A^*{=}j] = 0$,
+so $\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_T\mid A^*{=}j]=0$ and $\operatorname{Cov}(\varepsilon_T,\,g(A)\mid A^*{=}j)=0$ for every $j$.
+
+\emph{Step 2: Reduce the numerator.}
--
+\begin{align*}
+  D(C) &= \mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T\mid A^*)]
+       = V_W + \sigma_\varepsilon^2,
+\end{align*}
+where $\sigma_\varepsilon^2\equiv\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_T\mid A^*)]$.
+This is \emph{constant} (independent of $C$):
+$\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_T\mid A^*)]
+  = \sum_k p_k\,\operatorname{Var}(T\mid A{=}k)
+  = \mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T\mid A)]$,
--
+
+\emph{Step 4: Closed form and monotonicity.}
+$B(C) = \lambda\,V_W/(V_W+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)$,
+structurally identical to confounding (Proposition~\ref{prop:conf_prop}) with $\sigma_\varepsilon^2$ playing the role of $\sigma_T^2$.
+Since $V_W(\delta)$ is non-decreasing by DPI (same argument as \S\ref{app:monotonicity_proof}, Step~1) and $h(x)=|\lambda|x/(x+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)$ is increasing, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is non-decreasing.
+
+\paragraph{Boundedness of the leakage coefficient.}
--
+\emph{(a),(b).} With $\tau_{\mathrm{ind}}\neq 0$, $\lambda(C)\leq 1\iff B(C)/\tau_{\mathrm{ind}}\geq 0\iff \operatorname{sign}(B(C))=\operatorname{sign}(\tau_{\mathrm{ind}})$. Given (a), $\lambda(C)\geq 0\iff B(C)/\tau_{\mathrm{ind}}\leq 1\iff|B(C)|\leq|\tau_{\mathrm{ind}}|$.
+
+\emph{Proportional case.} Step~4 of the mediation derivation gives $B(C)=\lambda_0\,V_W(C)/(V_W(C)+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)$; the rank-one limit $V_W{\to}V$ yields $\tau_{\mathrm{ind}}=\lambda_0\,V/(V+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)$. Both quantities carry $\operatorname{sign}(\lambda_0)$, establishing~(a). The ratio
+\[
+  \frac{B(C)}{\tau_{\mathrm{ind}}}\;=\;\frac{V_W(C)\,(V+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)}{V\,(V_W(C)+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)}
+\]
+is monotone increasing in $V_W(C)\in[0,V]$ with range $[0,1]$ (its derivative in $V_W$ is $\sigma_\varepsilon^2(V+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)/[V(V_W+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)^2]>0$), establishing~(b). Hence $\lambda(C)\in[0,1]$ unconditionally.
+
+\emph{General case.} Substituting $\lambda(C)$ from~\eqref{eq:lambda_def} into~\eqref{eq:mediation} yields $\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}(C)=\lambda(C)\,\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}+(1{-}\lambda(C))\,\tau_{\mathrm{total}}$, so $\lambda(C)\in[0,1]$ iff $\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}(C)$ lies in the closed interval with endpoints $\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}$ and $\tau_{\mathrm{total}}$.
--
+which satisfies $\lambda_V(C)\in[0,1]$ \emph{unconditionally} by the law of total variance applied to $g(A)$. Under proportional effects, $\lambda$ and $\lambda_V$ agree at the endpoints and are related by
```

   - **λ(C) leakage coefficient**: Defined as V_B(C)/V in appendix. Proved ∈ [0,1] via law of total variance. method.tex conditions it on sign preservation sign(τ_dir) = sign(τ_total).    - **EMNLP 2026 rules**: 8-page main text, unlimited references, Limitations exempt from page count, should cover ~3 points
   - **ε sensitivity to DGP parameters**: ε < 0.06 violated at small τ (confounding/mediation max_ε ~0.12-0.13 at τ=0.1) and at large β scaling for front-door (max_ε=0.139 at τ=5.0)

```json
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe foundations are established in \\citet{carroll2006}, \\citet{gustafson2004}, \\citet{buonaccorsi2010}, and \\citet{bound2001measurement}, with starred-node DAGs formalized by \\citet{hernan_cole2009}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases toward the null~\\cite{brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}, but this fails for polytomous exposures~\\cite{dosemeci1990, jurek2005, veirod_laake2001}. \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} showed dependent misclassification can bias in either direction, a concern that also drives mediation analyses with mismeasured mediators~\\cite{valeri_vanderweele2013}. All analyze a single DAG topology; ours is the first to characterize how the \\emph{same} confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation.}\n\\citet{grimmer2022} and \\citet{feder_etal2022} provide foundations for text-as-data and causal inference in NLP. \\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to classifier error; \\citet{teblunthuis2024} gave the most complete binary treatment ($K{=}2$, four DAGs); \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only confounding. On annotation quality, \\citet{gilardi2023chatgpt} and \\citet{ziems2024can} document the rapid adoption of LLM annotators, \\citet{baumann2025} found 31\\% incorrect conclusions, \\citet{pangakis_wolken_fasching2023} demonstrated that automated annotations require task-specific validation, \\citet{cheng_mayya_sedoc2024} propose structured approaches to reduce LLM measurement error, and \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models. \\citet{audinet2025} benchmark debiasing; \\citet{keith_jensen_oconnor2020} survey the broader landscape. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose quality without connecting it to causal validity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and correction methods.}\nOur ASV framework draws on E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017}, the robustness value of \\citet{cinelli2020making}, and the mediation sensitivity analysis of \\citet{imai_keele_yamamoto2010}, but parametrizes annotation error severity rather than omitted variable strength or sequential ignorability violations. The unified measurement-error and missing-data framework of \\citet{blackwell_honaker_king2017} similarly motivates treating annotation noise as a first-class object of inference. For correction, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic; \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extend PPI to gold-free settings, and \\citet{gligoric2025} leverage LLM confidence scores. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four topologies. Bayesian measurement error models~\\cite{richardson1993bayesian, gustafson2004} and EM imputation offer alternatives that bypass FWL invariance.\n",
```

```json
{

    "type": "file",
```

```json
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology across seven canonical topologies. The classical binary guarantee breaks at $K \\geq 3$: exposure and IV topologies produce sign reversal, while five topologies maintain a structural guarantee. The ASV framework reveals a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four topologies due to FWL invariance. Empirical validation with 23 real LLM configurations confirms: the primary treatment effect never reverses, but 61\\% of exposure configurations reverse a secondary coefficient. Differential misclassification and correlated multi-LLM errors are natural extensions. Code for ASV computation and all experiments will be publicly released.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur framework assumes non-differential misclassification ($P(A^*|A, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*|A)$), which holds when the LLM does not observe $Y$ but may be violated when it accesses $Y$-correlated information---e.g., outcome-conditional prompting, few-shot demonstrations encoding outcome--treatment associations, or retrieval-augmented annotation carrying outcome signal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}). The framework assumes a correctly specified causal DAG; when the topology is uncertain, practitioners should compute ASV under candidate DAGs and report the most conservative. Closed-form expressions are derived for $K{=}3$ and verified at $K{=}5,7$ under linear structural equations; extensions to arbitrary $K$ and nonlinear models (Appendix~\\ref{sec:logistic_dgp}) remain open. We address misclassification of $A$ by a single annotator; outcome misclassification and correlated multi-annotator settings~\\cite{kim_garg2025} introduce additional bias channels not captured here.\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
```

```text
404:\emph{Proportional case.} Step~4 of the mediation derivation gives $B(C)=\lambda_0\,V_W(C)/(V_W(C)+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)$; the rank-one limit $V_W{\to}V$ yields $\tau_{\mathrm{ind}}=\lambda_0\,V/(V+\sigma_\varepsilon^2)$. Both quantities carry $\operatorname{sign}(\lambda_0)$, establishing~(a). The ratio
451:Unlike confounding, $\varepsilon_Y$ is not independent of $A$ (since $A$ depends on $Y=\tau T+\varepsilon_Y$), so Lemma~\ref{lem:bias_decomp} does not apply directly.
689:All conditions are on DGP parameters (not on $C$): once satisfied, sign preservation holds for all column-stochastic $C$ with $C_{kk}>0$.}
699:The sign preservation conditions for confounding, mediation, collider, and front-door are conditions on the DGP parameters (the relative strength of the causal effect versus the confounding, indirect, or collider-induced bias), not on the confusion matrix $C$.
702:For the DGP parameters used throughout our experiments, the conditions are comfortably satisfied.
845:[Omitted long matching line]
980:\subsection{ASV Stability Across DGP Parameters}
1016:[Omitted long matching line]
1018:\paragraph{DGP parameter sensitivity.}
1019:[Omitted long matching line]
1024:\caption{$\varepsilon \equiv \max_\delta(|B(\delta{+}\Delta)|{-}|B(\delta)|)/|\tau_{\text{true}}|$ in the non-monotonic direction (max over $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; 10{,}000 random $C_0$ per cell). Bold marks $\varepsilon > 0.06$. Counts in parentheses are violations / 30{,}000.}
```

```text
975	
976	For HumAID ($K{=}10$, 56 scenarios), 55.4\% are flagged as magnitude-ASV-unsafe---IV, mediation, front-door, and exposure all trigger, while confounding, collider, and M-bias remain below 10\% bias (Figure~\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). For CivilComments ($K{=}2$), 100\% of scenarios are ASV-unsafe despite $F_1$ ranging from $0.69$ (7B) to $0.82$ (frontier).
977	
978	Overlaying all 23 empirical matrices onto the theoretical Dirichlet violation-rate curves (Figure~\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay}) confirms placement: VAST in the danger zone, HumAID mid-range, CivilComments approaching safe for exposure but still in IV amplification.
979	
980	\subsection{ASV Stability Across DGP Parameters}
981	\label{sec:asv_stability}
982	
983	We test whether ASV rankings are stable across different data-generating process (DGP) configurations by varying the treatment-effect vector $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ across three settings: alternating-large ($\boldsymbol{\beta}=[0,\,1.0,\,-0.5]$), same-sign ($\boldsymbol{\beta}=[0,\,0.5,\,0.3]$), and alternating-small ($\boldsymbol{\beta}=[0,\,0.2,\,-0.1]$), with $K{=}3$, $\tau{=}0.5$, and five $C_0$ diagonal values $\in\{0.5,0.6,0.7,0.8,0.9\}$.
984	
985	\input{exp_e_asv_stability_table.tex}
986	
987	Three patterns emerge (Table~\ref{tab:asv_stability}). First, IV magnitude ASV is identical across all three DGPs ($\delta^*{=}0.203$). This is a mathematical identity, not an empirical finding: the Wald estimator $\hat{\tau}_{\text{IV}} = \text{RF}/\text{FS}$ has $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ in both numerator and denominator, and the ratio depends only on $C$ and the first-stage structure, making ASV invariant to $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ by construction. Second, exposure ASV depends on the coefficient ratio structure: alternating-large ($\beta_2/\beta_1 = -0.5$) and alternating-small ($\beta_2/\beta_1 = -0.5$) share the same ratio and yield identical $\delta^*{=}0.090$, while same-sign ($\beta_2/\beta_1 = 0.6$) gives $\delta^*{=}0.223$---thus the experiment provides two distinct sign-structure conditions, not three. Third, confounding ASV depends on effect magnitudes: only alternating-large triggers the 10\% threshold ($\delta^*{=}0.348$); smaller effects never reach 10\% bias.
988	
989	Confounding is consistently the most robust topology (highest ASV or no violation), and M-bias remains immune (not shown). However, the relative fragility of exposure vs.\ IV inverts with coefficient sign structure: under alternating signs, exposure is most fragile ($0.090 < 0.203$); under same-sign effects, IV is most fragile ($0.203 < 0.223$). Practitioners should compute ASV with their specific DGP rather than relying on generic rankings.
990	
991	\subsection{Extended Conjecture Verification}
992	\label{sec:conjecture_verify}
993	
994	We test envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}) by sampling 10{,}000 random $C_0$ matrices from Dirichlet$(1,\dots,1)$ per topology for $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (210{,}000 total), sweeping $\delta$ in steps of 0.01, and counting matrices with at least one non-monotonic step exceeding tolerance $10^{-12}$. Bias at each $\delta$ is computed via analytic probability limits using Gauss--Hermite quadrature (100 points), eliminating sampling noise entirely.
995	
996	\begin{table}[h]
997	\centering
998	\small
999	\caption{Envelope monotonicity violations per 10{,}000 random $C_0$ matrices. ``Worst mag.''\ is the largest single-step deviation $|B(\delta_{i+1})|-|B(\delta_i)|$ in the non-monotonic direction.}
1000	\label{tab:conjecture_verify}
1001	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
1002	\toprule
1003	\textbf{Topology} & $K{=}3$ & $K{=}5$ & $K{=}7$ & \textbf{Worst mag.} & \textbf{Direction} \\
1004	\midrule
1005	Confounding & 8467 & 8063 & 7892 & 0.011 & non-dec \\
1006	Mediation   & 8236 & 7905 & 7893 & 0.013 & non-dec \\
1007	Collider    & 8683 & 7669 & 7570 & 0.005 & non-inc \\
1008	Exposure    & 485  & 205  & 77   & 0.056 & non-dec \\
1009	M-bias      & 9791 & 7783 & 7399 & 0.002 & non-inc \\
1010	IV          & 7433 & 6015 & 5711 & $>10^6$ & non-dec \\
1011	Front-door  & 8429 & 8079 & 7899 & 0.013 & non-dec \\
1012	\bottomrule
1013	\end{tabular}
1014	\end{table}
1015	
1016	Violation counts are high (60--97\% of matrices), but magnitudes are negligible for non-IV topologies: all worst-case deviations fall below 0.06 ($<$6\% of the respective $\tau$). These results are computed from analytic probability limits with zero sampling noise, confirming the violations are structural properties of multi-class confusion matrices rather than numerical artifacts. IV deviations reflect Wald estimator singularities when the first-stage coefficient passes near zero---this is a property of the Wald estimator, not of the ASV parameterization. Adversarial search (500 restarts per configuration, $K \in \{3,5\}$) finds genuine counterexamples for all topologies except IV $K{=}3$, confirming that strict monotonicity does not hold universally. The bias envelope is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic with $\varepsilon < 0.06$ for all six non-IV topologies at the default $\tau$ values. Empirically, $\varepsilon$ scales as $O(1/K^\alpha)$ with $\alpha \in [0.6, 1.5]$ (topology-dependent): for confounding, mediation, and front-door, $\alpha \approx 1.4$; for exposure, $\alpha \approx 1.0$; for collider and M-bias, $\alpha \approx 0.7$. A universal empirical upper bound is $\varepsilon \leq 0.137/\sqrt{K}$, verified across all tested configurations ($K \in \{3,5,7\}$). This scaling supports the practical validity of ASV as a sensitivity tool at any meaningful bias threshold.
1017	
1018	\paragraph{DGP parameter sensitivity.}
1019	We repeat the verification across $\tau \in \{0.1, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, 5.0\}$, varying the direct $T{\to}Y$ coefficient for confounding ($\beta_T{=}\tau$), mediation, collider, and M-bias (DGP $\tau$), and rescaling the $A{\to}Y$ coefficient vector to $\boldsymbol{\beta}\cdot\tau$ for exposure and front-door. With 10{,}000 random $C_0$ per $(K,\tau)$ setting (Table~\ref{tab:conjecture_tau_sens}), the bound $\varepsilon{<}0.06$ holds uniformly for $\tau \geq 0.5$ for confounding, mediation, collider, and M-bias, but breaks down at $\tau{=}0.1$: mediation produces 1{,}950 / 30{,}000 violations across $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (worst $\varepsilon{=}0.130$ at $K{=}3$) and confounding 200 / 30{,}000 (worst $\varepsilon{=}0.118$ at $K{=}3$). The mechanism is dimensional: additive terms not scaling with $\tau$ (e.g.\ the $\boldsymbol{\beta}$-contribution to $\mathbb{E}[Y D_k]$ in mediation and confounding) contribute a $\tau$-independent offset to the bias numerator, so the ratio $|B|/|\tau|$ inflates as $\tau \to 0$. Collider and M-bias remain robust at all $\tau$ ($\varepsilon{<}0.022$ and $\varepsilon{<}0.015$ respectively). Exposure shows scattered tail outliers but $\overline{\varepsilon} \approx 3\!\times\!10^{-4}$ at every $\tau$, consistent with linear scaling: when $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ rescales by $\tau$, both bias and $\tau_{\text{true}}$ scale by the same factor and $\varepsilon$ is theoretically invariant. Front-door inverts the pattern ($\varepsilon$ \emph{grows} with $\tau$, reaching $0.139$ at $\tau{=}5$, $K{=}3$, with 664 violations) because $\tau_{\text{true}} = \operatorname{plim}(\hat{\tau}_T \mid C{=}I)$ is pinned at $0.5$ by the $U$-confounding path while bias magnitudes scale with $\boldsymbol{\beta}$---this is an artefact of how $\tau_{\text{true}}$ is normalised, not a genuine failure of envelope monotonicity. In practice, ASV's $\delta^*$ thresholds (typically $\geq 0.1$ in our deployment) are well above the region where these $\varepsilon$-violations occur, so the small-$\tau$ degradation does not affect ASV's operational reliability; we flag it as a caveat for applications targeting $|\tau| \lesssim 0.1$ in standardised units.
1020	
1021	\begin{table}[h]
1022	\centering
1023	\small
1024	\caption{$\varepsilon \equiv \max_\delta(|B(\delta{+}\Delta)|{-}|B(\delta)|)/|\tau_{\text{true}}|$ in the non-monotonic direction (max over $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; 10{,}000 random $C_0$ per cell). Bold marks $\varepsilon > 0.06$. Counts in parentheses are violations / 30{,}000.}
1025	\label{tab:conjecture_tau_sens}
1026	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
1027	\toprule
1028	\textbf{Topology} & $\tau{=}0.1$ & $\tau{=}0.5$ & $\tau{=}1.0$ & $\tau{=}2.0$ & $\tau{=}5.0$ \\
1029	\midrule
1030	Confounding & \textbf{0.118} (200) & 0.027 (0) & 0.014 (0) & 0.007 (0) & 0.002 (0) \\
1031	Mediation   & \textbf{0.130} (1950) & 0.026 (0) & 0.013 (0) & \textbf{0.112} (1) & 0.003 (0) \\
1032	Collider    & 0.022 (0) & 0.006 (0) & 0.005 (0) & 0.004 (0) & 0.002 (0) \\
1033	Exposure    & \textbf{0.111} (5) & \textbf{0.122} (2) & \textbf{0.152} (2) & \textbf{0.089} (3) & \textbf{0.148} (3) \\
1034	M-bias      & 0.014 (0) & 0.003 (0) & 0.001 (0) & 0.001 (0) & 0.000 (0) \\
1035	Front-door  & 0.003 (0) & 0.013 (0) & 0.027 (0) & 0.051 (0) & \textbf{0.139} (664) \\
1036	\bottomrule
1037	\end{tabular}
1038	\end{table}

1041	\label{app:notation_table}
1042
```

```text
88	\end{theorem}
89	
90	\emph{Proof sketch.} Both topologies reduce to $B(C(\delta)) {=} \lambda V_W(\delta)/(V_W(\delta) {+} \sigma^2)$ where $V_W(\delta) {=} \operatorname{Var}(g) {-} \operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g{\mid}A^*_\delta])$ and $\sigma^2$ is a topology-specific constant ($\sigma_T^2$ for confounding, $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T{\mid}A)]$ for mediation). As $\delta$ increases, $A^*$ becomes a noisier channel for $A$, so $\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g{\mid}A^*_\delta])$ decreases by the data processing inequality, increasing $V_W$. Since $x/(x{+}c)$ is increasing for $c{>}0$, $|B|$ is non-decreasing (full proofs in Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof} and~\ref{par:med_monotone}). For general (non-proportional) effects, numerical verification across 10{,}000 random matrices confirms $\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity with $\varepsilon < 0.02$ (Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof}).
91	
92	\begin{conjecture}[$\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]
93	\label{conj:monotone}
94	For the four remaining non-IV topologies (exposure, collider, M-bias, front-door) under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$: exposure and front-door are $\varepsilon$-approximately non-decreasing in $\delta$, and collider and M-bias (protective topologies) are $\varepsilon$-approximately non-increasing in $\delta$, with $\varepsilon < 0.06$ ($<$6\% of $\tau$). Mediation envelope monotonicity is proved for proportional effects (Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}) and verified numerically for the general case with $\varepsilon < 0.02$. Verified across 10{,}000 random matrices per topology and $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ using analytic probability limits (Appendix~\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}).
95	\end{conjecture}
96	
97	The IV topology is excluded from Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}: the Wald estimator exhibits singularities when the first-stage coefficient passes through zero, producing unbounded deviations. Magnitude ASV remains well-defined away from these singularities.
98	
99	When strict monotonicity holds only approximately ($\varepsilon < 0.06$), the reported $\delta^*$ may differ from the true threshold by at most $\varepsilon/\eta$ in $\delta$-units---negligible at the recommended $\eta = 10\%$. Topology rankings are preserved across all tested configurations.
100	
101	Binarization of $K{=}3$ annotations introduces additional topology-dependent bias (Appendix~\ref{app:binarization}).
102
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full exposure data. Key observations:

**Exposure topology**: The tau_param_mapping says "beta *= tau (linear in tau; eps invariant)" — meaning exposure is supposed to have τ-invariant ε because both bias and τ_true scale by the same factor. The data confirms this:

| τ | max_ε (aggregate) | violations | mean_ε |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.1 | 0.111 | 5 | 0.000149 |


| 0.5 | 0.122 | 2 | 0.000144 |
| 1.0 | 0.152 | 2 | 0.000147 |
| 2.0 | 0.089 | 3 | 0.000138 |
| 5.0 | 0.148 | 3 | 0.000147 |

The mean ε stays remarkably stable around 0.00015 across all τ values, confirming the τ-invariance property, though the maximum ε shows more variability ranging from 0.089 to 0.152.

The violations are sparse—only 2-5 out of 30,000 cases—and cluster almost entirely at K=3, suggesting these are extreme tail outliers. However, this creates a tension with the original conjecture: while the default τ case showed a worst-case exposure of 0.056, the τ sweep reveals that several tail matrices exceed the stated 0.06 threshold, which contradicts the claim in the method that exposure stays below 6% of τ.

The discrepancy might stem from differences in how the metrics are computed—Table 9 measures raw deviation magnitude while Table 10 normalizes by the true τ value, or there could be differences in the β parameterization between the two analyses. So ε is actually the normalized deviation divided by τ_true, not the raw value—Conjecture 1's statement of "ε < 0.06 (<6% of τ)" confirms this is a fractional bound. Table 9 shows the raw deviation of 0.056 for exposure, which at τ = 0.5 would give a normalized value around 0.112, roughly matching Table 10's 0.122 entry.

But there's a discrepancy in how the paper frames this—line 1016 says "all worst-case deviations fall below 0.06 (<6% of the respective τ)" which seems to treat 0.06 as the raw deviation threshold, yet that interpretation doesn't align with the actual normalized definition. I need to figure out what the default τ value is to reconcile these numbers. I'm seeing the key distinction now: Table 9 reports raw deviations while Table 10 normalizes by τ_true. The conjecture defines ε as a normalized metric (deviation/τ), so if the raw worst-case deviation is below 0.06 and τ=1, that matches the 6% threshold, but the relationship breaks down when τ differs from 1.

Looking at the exposure parameter mapping, it claims ε should be τ-invariant, yet the data shows max_ε ranging from 0.089 to 0.152 across different τ values—though the mean stays remarkably stable around 0.00015. This variation in the extremes likely stems from different random seeds producing different outlier matrices, so with only a handful of violations out of 30,000 trials, individual tail events dominate the maximum rather than reflecting a systematic pattern.

Now I need to reconcile Table 9 and Table 10: if both use τ=1.0, Table 9 shows exposure worst magnitude of 0.056 (giving ε=0.056), but Table 10 shows max_ε=0.152 with 2 violations. That's a significant discrepancy unless the tables are measuring something different or using different data.

Looking at the methodology, Table 9 samples 10,000 random C_0 matrices per topology across K values (210,000 total), while Table 10 samples 10,000 random C_0 per (K,τ) combination. They likely used different random seeds, which explains why Table 9 happened to draw matrices with max deviation 0.056 while Table 10's independent draw produced 0.152. Since exposure violations are rare tail events, different random samples naturally produce different extremes. This means the ε < 0.06 claim for exposure is actually seed-dependent—the conjecture as stated is too strong because a different random draw yields violations.

Now I need to investigate the mediation anomaly at τ=2.0.
```

```text
Now I have the full picture. Let me summarize the findings for the W9 review:

**Mediation at τ=2.0**: max_ε = 0.112, 1 violation out of 30,000 (K=3 only). Mean_ε = 0.0016. So this is a single outlier matrix at K=3. The p99 is only 0.005. This is a genuine but extremely rare tail event.

**Key cross-verification findings:**

1. **Exposure has ε > 0.06 at ALL τ values** (Table 10 shows 0.111, 0.122, 0.152, 0.089, 0.148). However:
   - Only 2-5 violations out of 30,000 (< 0.02% violation rate)
   - Mean ε ≈ 0.00015 (truly τ-invariant)
   - All violations at K=3 (mostly) or K=5
   - This is a tail phenomenon: 99th percentile ε ≈ 0.008, well below 0.06

2. **Conjecture 1 inconsistency**: The main text (method.tex line 94) says "ε < 0.06" without qualification. But Table 9 shows this claim needs refinement—the violations are concentrated in extreme tail cases at specific K values, not systematic failures.

3. **Mediation at τ=2.0**: A single extreme outlier (1/30,000) with ε = 0.112 sits far beyond the 99th percentile of 0.005, suggesting this is a rare edge case rather than a pattern violation.

4. **Front-door τ=5.0**: At this parameter value, violations spike to 664 cases with max ε = 0.139, indicating the conjecture breaks down more substantially at higher τ settings.

The normalization effect in front-door is well-explained: τ_true stays fixed at 0.5 due to U-confounding while bias scales with β, which the appendix documents clearly. Collider and M-bias remain consistently safe across all tests. Confounding at τ=0.1 produces 200 violations with max ε = 0.118, which aligns with the dimensional argument provided.

The core problem is that Conjecture 1's claim about ε < 0.06 for the four non-IV topologies is demonstrably false for exposure—Table 10 shows violations across every DGP parameter tested. The appendix acknowledges exposure has "scattered tail outliers" but then cites a mean of approximately 3×10⁻⁴, which masks the actual violations occurring in the tails.

The real issue is that the conjecture bounds the maximum error, but the appendix switches to discussing the mean, which are fundamentally different claims. When β rescales by τ, the text argues both bias and τ_true scale proportionally so ε should be invariant, yet the data shows exposure max_ε reaches 0.152 at τ=1.0—far exceeding the 0.06 bound stated in the conjecture. This inconsistency needs to be flagged as a warning-level discrepancy between the theoretical claim and empirical results.

The review should also note the isolated violation in mediation at τ=2.0 where one matrix out of 30,000 hits ε=0.112, which the appendix doesn't address. Additionally, there's an inconsistency between how Table 9 and Table 10 handle exposure data that needs clarification.

Looking at the tables more carefully, Table 9 reports a worst magnitude of 0.056 at default τ, but Table 10 at τ=0.5 shows max_ε = 0.122. The discrepancy stems from different random seeds for C_0, which means the "ε < 0.06" bound doesn't hold uniformly across all configurations for exposure. The appendix does well explaining the dimensional mechanism behind small-τ degradation and correctly identifies the front-door normalization artifact, with an appropriate caveat for |τ| ≲ 0.1.

The ASV δ* thresholds stay above 0.1, which is well outside the violation region, so the method maintains operational reliability in practice.

Reconsidering the exposure scaling more carefully: the tau_param_mapping scales beta linearly with tau, so if default beta = [0, 1.0, -0.5] and tau = 0.5, the actual beta used becomes [0, 0.5, -0.25]. Since the bias formula depends only on how the confusion matrix affects A*'s marginal distribution, the bias scales with beta, and tau_true also scales with beta. This means ε = max_deviation/tau_true should be τ-invariant. The mean ε across experiments is indeed τ-invariant at around 0.00015.

The variation in maximum ε across different random seeds just reflects sampling variability in the tail behavior of the confusion matrices. Looking at the full 150,000 exposure tests (5 τ values × 30,000 matrices), the true ε is likely around 0.15 — the observed maximum is 0.152. This means the conjecture's ε < 0.06 bound is too tight for exposure; the universal bound should be ε < 0.16 to cover it, or exposure needs separate treatment. Checking the original Table 9, at default τ with 10,000 matrices per K, the worst single-step deviation for exposure was 0.056, with 485 matrices (4.85%) at K=3 exceeding the threshold.

But Table 10 at τ=1.0 shows max_ε = 0.152 for the same K=3, using a different set of 10,000 random matrices — the extreme outlier wasn't hit in Table 9's sample but appeared in Table 10's. The ε < 0.06 claim was calibrated on Table 9's particular sample, so this is a reproducibility issue: the bound doesn't hold across different random samples and should be stated probabilistically instead.

Let me verify what each table is actually measuring — Table 9 reports raw deviation while Table 10 normalizes by τ_true, so I need to check whether they're directly comparable given the default τ value. The key insight here is that Table 9's ε < 0.06 result for exposure is actually quite sensitive to the random sample—when I look at Table 10 with τ=1.0 using a different random draw, the maximum deviation jumps to 0.152, which shows this isn't a robust finding across independent samples. The discrepancy comes entirely from different random C_0 matrices being generated. Now I'm ready to write up the review.
```

```json
{
  "text": "## 审查报告 — W9 ε-DGP sensitivity (exp_d_epsilon_tau_sensitivity)\n\n**结论**: WARNING\n\n### 发现\n\n**1. [severity: warning] Conjecture 1 对 exposure 拓扑技术上不成立**\n\nConjecture 1（method.tex L94）声称\"四个非IV拓扑 ε < 0.06\"。但 Table 10（appendix_proofs.tex L1033）显示 **exposure 在所有 τ 值下 max_ε 均超过 0.06**：\n\n| τ | max_ε | violations/30k |\n|---|---|---|\n| 0.1 | 0.111 | 5 |\n| 0.5 | 0.122 | 2 |\n| 1.0 | **0.152** | 2 |\n| 2.0 | 0.089 | 3 |\n| 5.0 | 0.148 | 3 |\n\n违规率极低（< 0.02%），mean_ε ≈ 0.00015（τ-invariant），p99 ≈ 0.008。这是极端尾部现象——但 Conjecture 1 约束的是 max，不是 mean 或 p99。\n\n更关键的是：Table 9（原始验证，L1008）显示 exposure worst mag = 0.056 < 0.06，而 Table 10 在 τ=1.0（应与默认一致）显示 max_ε = 0.152。**两个表用不同随机 C_0 样本，ε < 0.06 的结论对 exposure 是 seed-dependent 的**。\n\n建议三选一：\n- (a) 将 Conjecture 1 限定为\"confounding, mediation (τ ≥ 0.5), collider, M-bias, front-door (τ ≤ 2)\"——exposure 单独处理为\"ε < 0.06 with probability > 99.98%\"\n- (b) 放宽 bound 至 ε < 0.16 以覆盖 exposure 尾部\n- (c) 在 Conjecture 1 中注明 exposure 的 ε bound 是 probabilistic（p99.98 < 0.06）而非 worst-case\n\n**2. [severity: warning] Mediation τ=2.0 孤立违规未在正文讨论**\n\nJSON 显示 mediation τ=2.0 有 1/30,000 违规（K=3, max_ε=0.112, p99=0.005）。Table 10（L1031）正确标粗。但 appendix 段落（L1019）只讨论了 τ=0.1 的降解机制，**未解释 τ=2.0 的孤立违规**。这不是 small-τ dimensional 问题，机制不同——可能是特定 C_0 矩阵导致 mediation 路径的 partial cancellation。应在 paragraph 中加一句说明。\n\n**3. [severity: info] Appendix 分析质量良好**\n\nDGP sensitivity paragraph（L1018-1019）正确识别了：\n- Small-τ dimensional 机制（additive terms 不随 τ 缩放）\n- Front-door normalization artifact（τ_true pinned at 0.5）\n- Exposure 的 ε 理论上 τ-invariant（mean 确认，max 因 sampling 波动）\n- 实际影响有限（ASV δ* ≥ 0.1 高于违规区域）\n- 对 |τ| ≲ 0.1 的 caveat 恰当\n\nTable 10 数据与 JSON 交叉验证一致（confounding 0.118、mediation 0.130/0.112、exposure 全 bold、collider/M-bias safe、front-door 0.139）。\n\n**4. [severity: info] Table 9 与 Table 10 使用不同 C_0 样本**\n\nTable 9 的 exposure worst mag = 0.056 vs Table 10 τ=1.0 的 max_ε = 0.152，差异来自不同随机样本。对 collider/M-bias/confounding/mediation（高违规率 60-97%）影响小，但对 exposure（违规率 < 0.02%）max 统计量对样本敏感。建议在 appendix 中注明两表使用独立随机种子。\n\n### 建议\n\n优先处理发现 #1——Conjecture 1 的 exposure 问题影响论文核心理论贡献的严谨性。推荐方案 (a)：保持 ε < 0.06 对 5 个拓扑的 worst-case claim（排除 exposure），exposure 单独表述为 probabilistic bound。这样最诚实，也最不易被审稿人攻击。",
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```
